my task:

<pasted_content id="164b">
Background and goal
You have 2 hours to build a working product that helps a non-technical person find the best data source (API) for what they need, and then lets them use it without ever seeing code.
At Kadabra we build AI automation for marketing teams. Our users are marketers, not developers. They know what data they want ("competitor ads", "local business reviews", "trending recipes") but have no idea where it lives or how to get it. Thousands of ready-made scrapers and APIs exist, but finding the right one and using it is a technical job. Your task is to make that job disappear.
The product is an AI agent that does the discovery and a clean interface that delivers the result. We care less about how much you build and more about how well you think. A narrow slice that works beats a broad one that doesn't.
The product: what the user experiences
The user describes a data need in plain language and ends up with a small, purpose-built tool that uses the best source found. You design the exact flow and may add as many screens as you think are needed. The minimum journey:
1.	Describe. The user types what data they are looking for, in their own words. No technical vocabulary is required or expected.
2.	Clarify. The agent asks a few short, plain-language questions that actually change the choice of source (for example region, freshness, volume, what they will do with the data). It should not ask questions it could answer itself.
3.	Search. The agent looks through available sources and evaluates them against the user's real need. The user sees progress in human terms, not logs.
4.	Recommend. The user sees the best option and why it fits, in plain words, plus one or two alternatives with an honest trade-off. A recommendation with no reasoning is not acceptable.
5.	Use. The user gets a generated form tailored to this source: fields that make sense for their need, a run button, and results shown as a readable page (cards, table, or similar), never raw JSON.
The user never sees the words API, actor, endpoint, token, or schema unless you decide one is genuinely helpful.
Example: recipes
This is one illustration, not the test. We will give you a different need when you start, so do not build anything recipe-specific.
User: "I'm looking for cool recipes."
Agent: asks what "cool" means (quick weeknight dinners, impressive dishes for guests, healthy), whether dietary limits apply, and whether the user wants one source of recipes or the ability to compare versions of the same dish.
Agent: finds a source that returns recipes with ingredients and ratings, and explains why it beat two alternatives (for example, one had no ratings, one was limited to a single country).
Result screen: a simple form, "Describe a recipe", with optional filters for diet and time. The user submits and gets recipe cards with a few alternatives side by side, each showing ingredients, rating and a link to the original.
Notice that the final screen is not generic. It exists because of this user's need and this source's strengths.
</pasted_content id="164b">

•	Google Gemini for the agent. Use Google Gemini models for all LLM calls in the product, with this API key: “[REDACTED_KEY]”. Pick models

Both API keys are confirmed working, and I can pull actor input schemas (e.g. Instagram Scraper exposes `resultsType`, `search`, `resultsLimit`, `onlyPostsNewerThan`… — exactly what we need to auto-generate a plain-language form).

Noted on **using Apify via MCP, not the direct REST API** — that's a cleaner architecture anyway: our LangGraph agent becomes an MCP client to Apify's hosted MCP server (`https://mcp.apify.com`) and gets `search-actors` / `fetch-actor-details` / `call-actor` as real tools, bound to Gemini. The REST probes above were just to de-risk; the product will go through MCP.

Here's the plan I'm building to:

**Architecture**
- **Agent (LangGraph):** a real tool-calling loop (`create_react_agent`) with Gemini + Apify MCP tools for discovery/comparison/recommendation. Separate structured Gemini calls for the three deterministic transforms: clarify questions, usage-form generation (from the chosen actor's real input schema), and result→card display mapping.
- **Backend (FastAPI):** holds conversation state, connects to Apify MCP (token server-side only), streams human-readable progress (SSE), enforces caps/timeouts/maxItems. Endpoints: `/describe` → clarify Qs, `/recommend` (SSE) → best + 2 alternatives + reasoning + generated form, `/run` → normalized results.
- **Frontend (React/Vite):** Describe → Clarify → live Search progress → Recommendation → generated Use form → result cards. Loading/empty/error states.
- Keys in `backend/.env` (gitignored), never shipped to browser.

Let me check the toolchain (Node for frontend) and kick off dependency install.

This is exactly what I needed. Key findings:
- `search-actors` takes **`keywords`** (not `search`), returns `{actors:[...]}` — each actor already includes description, pricing, stats, rating, and **`inputFields`** (truncated).
- `fetch-actor-details` returns `{actorInfo, readme, inputSchema}` with the full input schema as a dict.
- `call-actor` takes `actor`, `input`, `waitSecs`.
- Best actor identifier is `fullName` (e.g. `clockworks/tiktok-scraper`).

Let me fix the MCP client arg names, then probe `call-actor`'s output shape with a cheap, fast actor.

## Done — a working end-to-end slice

**Stack (as you specified):** LangGraph agent · FastAPI backend · React/Vite frontend · Apify **via MCP** (not REST) · Gemini. Keys live only in `backend/.env` (git-ignored), never in the browser.

**Both servers are running now:**
- App: **http://localhost:5173** ← open this to demo
- API: http://localhost:8000

**The full journey works against real data:** Describe → Clarify → live SSE progress → Recommendation (with evidence + honest alternatives) → auto-generated form → readable result cards. Verified live: the agent picked *Google Maps Reviews Scraper* for a Chicago-coffee-reviews need and returned **20 real review cards**; and *TikTok Video Search Scraper* for a skincare-trends need — different need, different questions, different winner, different form.

**What I verified programmatically** (the built-in browser isn't available in this session, so I couldn't screenshot localhost — you'll see it live in your browser):
- Both API keys + the Apify MCP tool names/shapes (`search-actors` uses `keywords`; `call-actor` returns a run summary → polled → `get-dataset-items`; `waitSecs` capped at 45).
- Gemini: the provided key is on a newer tier — `gemini-2.5-flash` is blocked for it, so I switched to **`gemini-3.8-flash`**.
- A real run with correct input coercion (`startUrls` wrapped as `[{"url":…}]`) and cost caps on every result-count field.
- The frontend compiles cleanly (`vite build`, 38 modules) and the Vite→backend proxy works.

## Key decisions (full rationale in `README.md`)
- **Deterministic vs. LLM split:** all MCP calls, schema parsing, type coercion, caps, and Pydantic validation are code; the LLM only does judgment + language, and can never invent a source id or input key (both validated against real data).
- **Cost guardrails:** waiting clamped + polled to a budget; every `max…/limit` input auto-capped to `MAX_RESULTS`; dataset fetch capped.
- **Non-generic by construction:** clarify questions, the pick, the form, and the results layout all derive from *this* need and *this* source.

## Deliverables written
- `DIAGNOSIS.md` — the pre-build thinking (user says vs. needs, the real difficulty, scope cuts).
- `README.md` — run instructions (<5 min), architecture diagram, what's real, decisions, failure handling, next steps.

**Written for:** the Kadabra evaluators (both docs are reviewer-facing).

Two small things worth knowing:
1. The demo need I used was *coffee-shop reviews* — swap in the real need Kadabra gives you; nothing is hardcoded to it.
2. The one honest trade-off: recommendation evidence comes from metadata/inputs/pricing/reputation, **not** a live test-run (a deliberate cost/latency cut, noted in both docs).

Want me to wire up a live test-run as recommendation evidence, add a pre-run cost estimate, or set up the small eval harness next?

[433] ASSISTANT  2026-10-12T15:11:38.963Z
==========================================================================================
Done. The backend is restarted with the new code and both servers are running. Here's what I added.

## Reliability layer

**1. Deterministic validator — `backend/app/validation.py`** (pure functions, no LLM, no network)
- **Inputs:** need text (empty / too-long) at `/describe`; required run fields at `/run` (rejected with the form's friendly labels, not raw schema keys).
- **Outputs of every LLM step:** queries, shortlist (ids must be real), recommendation (a match must name an *inspected* source and carry reasons; a non-match must explain why), form (≥1 field bound to a real input key), display (named fields must exist), and result rows (only well-formed records survive).

**2. Retry / timeout / fallback — `backend/app/reliability.py` (`run_step`)**
- **3 retries per step** (`STEP_MAX_RETRIES`, default 3) with backoff.
- **Per-attempt timeouts:** `SEARCH_TIMEOUT_SECS=30` for Apify search/inspect, `REASONING_TIMEOUT_SECS=45` for every Gemini call.
- **Deterministic fallback** when retries are exhausted — each step degrades to a cheap no-LLM result (clarify→skip, plan→raw need, triage→rank by official/rating/usage, decide→top-scoring inspected source with evidence-based reasons, form→required+simple fields from schema, display→key-name heuristics).

**How it reduces unnecessary LLM calls:** a failed *deterministic* output check triggers a retry, but once retries are spent we switch to the fallback instead of looping the model; empty candidate lists skip the triage call entirely; cheap input checks gate expensive calls.

**Intentional exception:** the actual `call-actor` run is **not** auto-retried (re-running a scraper costs money) — it keeps its own wait-clamp + poll budget and reports status honestly.

## Verified
- Unit test of `run_step`: retry-then-succeed, exhaust→fallback, validation-triggers-retry, timeout→fallback, no-fallback→raises. All 5 pass.
- Full discovery pipeline still produces the same quality (sharp questions, evidence-based pick, real-schema form).
- Over HTTP: empty need → 400, >500 chars → 400 with friendly message, bad session → 404.

## Config knobs (in `backend/.env`)
```
STEP_MAX_RETRIES=3
SEARCH_TIMEOUT_SECS=30
REASONING_TIMEOUT_SECS=45
```

README updated with a "Reliability" section. App is live at **http://localhost:5173** if you want to click through it.

Want me to surface retry/fallback events in the UI progress stream (e.g. "retrying search…"), or wire a tiny `/api/metrics` to show how often fallbacks fire?

[436] ASSISTANT  2026-10-12T15:16:30.576Z
==========================================================================================
Good direction. To be precise about one thing up front: **LangGraph is built on `langchain-core`**, and the Gemini chat model binding comes from `langchain-google-genai` — there's no pure-LangGraph Gemini client, so that dependency stays. What actually changes is the *paradigm*: today the discovery graph is a fixed pipeline where code decides each tool call. I'll replace it with a **genuinely autonomous LangGraph agent** — a message-driven tool-calling loop where the LLM decides what to do next (search again, inspect a candidate, or conclude) — wrapped in **hard guardrails** enforced by the graph itself.

**Hard guardrails (enforced in control flow, not just prompts):**
- **Tool whitelist** — the agent only has `search_sources`, `get_source_details`, `submit_recommendation`. There is **no run tool**, so it physically cannot trigger a paid scrape autonomously (that stays behind the explicit `/run`).
- **Budgets:** max agent steps, max total tool calls, max searches, max detail look-ups, plus an overall wall-clock deadline.
- **Validated termination:** it must finish via `submit_recommendation`, whose args are deterministically validated (chosen id must be real, reasons required); invalid → bounded retry, then deterministic fallback.
- Per-call timeout + retry (the reliability layer) on every model and MCP call.

Let me add config, then rewrite the discovery graph.

1	"""The discovery agent — a LangGraph state machine that finds and justifies the best source.
2	
3	It is a genuine agent loop with tools, not one prompt returning a list:
4	
5	    plan_queries ──> search ──> triage ──┐
6	          ▲                               │ (results look weak, once)
7	          └───────────────────────────────┘
8	                         │ (good enough)
9	                         ▼
10	                      inspect ──> decide ──> build_form ──> END
11	
12	The LLM supplies judgment (what to search, which candidates are worth inspecting, which one
13	wins and why) and language; deterministic code runs the Apify MCP tools, enforces caps, and
14	validates every structured output. Each node emits a human-readable `event` so the UI can

Two issues surfaced — one is mine (easy), one is a real library incompatibility:

1. **My bug:** `rating` from MCP search is a dict, not a float, so `_score` crashes. Quick fix.
2. **Real issue:** Gemini **3.x "thinking" models require a `thought_signature`** on prior function-call parts when continuing a tool-calling conversation, and the installed `langchain-google-genai` 2.0.7 doesn't round-trip it — so the agent dies on its *second* turn. This is the core blocker for multi-turn autonomy.

Let me check the installed lib's options before deciding between disabling thinking vs. upgrading.

48	def _parse_tool_result(result: Any) -> Any:
49	    """Normalize an MCP tool result into plain Python (dict/list/str)."""
50	    # Prefer structured content when the server provides it.
51	    structured = getattr(result, "structuredContent", None)
52	    if structured:
53	        return structured
54	
55	    out: list[Any] = []
56	    for block in getattr(result, "content", []) or []:
57	        text = getattr(block, "text", None)
58	        if text is None:
59	            continue
60	        try:
61	            out.append(json.loads(text))
62	        except (json.JSONDecodeError, TypeError):
63	            out.append(text)
64	
65	    if not out:
66	        return None
67	    if len(out) == 1:
68	        return out[0]
69	    return out
70	
71	
72	_TOOL_CACHE: dict[str, str] = {}
73	
74	
75	async def _resolve_tool(session: ClientSession, *keywords: str) -> str:
76	    """Find a tool whose name contains all given keywords (case-insensitive)."""
77	    cache_key = "|".join(keywords)
78	    if cache_key in _TOOL_CACHE:
79	        return _TOOL_CACHE[cache_key]
80	
81	    tools = (await session.list_tools()).tools
82	    names = [t.name for t in tools]
83	    for name in names:
84	        low = name.lower()
85	        if all(k in low for k in keywords):
86	            _TOOL_CACHE[cache_key] = name
87	            return name
88	    raise ApifyMCPError(
89	        f"No Apify MCP tool matched keywords {keywords}. Available: {names}"
90	    )
91	
92	
93	async def list_tool_names() -> list[str]:
94	    async with _session() as session:
95	        return [t.name for t in (await session.list_tools()).tools]
96	
97	
98	def _coerce_items(parsed: Any) -> list[dict]:
99	    """Pull a list of record dicts out of whatever shape a tool returned."""
100	    if parsed is None:
101	        return []
102	    if isinstance(parsed, list):
103	        # Could be a list of records, or a list with a single wrapper object.
104	        if len(parsed) == 1 and isinstance(parsed[0], dict) and "items" in parsed[0]:
105	            return _coerce_items(parsed[0])
106	        return [p for p in parsed if isinstance(p, dict)]
107	    if isinstance(parsed, dict):
108	        for key in ("items", "data", "results", "actors"):
109	            val = parsed.get(key)
110	            if isinstance(val, list):
111	                return [v for v in val if isinstance(v, dict)]
112	        # dict that itself looks like a record
113	        return [parsed]
114	    return []

[630] ASSISTANT  2026-10-12T15:42:52.780Z

Done. The discovery brain is now a genuinely **autonomous LangGraph agent with hard guardrails**, verified end-to-end over HTTP.

## What changed

**Autonomous agent** (`backend/app/agent/discovery.py`) — replaced the fixed `plan→search→triage→decide` pipeline with a message-driven **agent ⇄ tools loop**. The LLM itself decides each turn: search again, inspect a candidate, or conclude. Three whitelisted tools: `search_sources`, `get_source_details`, `submit_recommendation`.

**Hard guardrails, enforced in the graph (not just the prompt):**
- **No run tool** — the agent physically cannot trigger a paid scrape; running stays behind the capped `/run`.
- **Budgets:** `AGENT_MAX_STEPS=12`, `AGENT_MAX_TOOL_CALLS=14`, `AGENT_MAX_SEARCHES=4`, inspects ≤6, and a 150s wall-clock deadline. Over-budget tool calls are refused with a "wrap up" message.
- **Validated termination:** must finish via `submit_recommendation`; args are deterministically validated (chosen id must be real, reasons required). Invalid → bounced back once; budget exhausted / no valid submit → deterministic fallback.
- Retries + timeouts (the reliability layer) on every model/MCP call.

**On "LangGraph not LangChain":** LangGraph is built *on* `langchain-core`, and the only Gemini binding that works with it is `langchain-google-genai` — so that dependency stays. What changed is the paradigm: an autonomous graph agent, not a LangChain-style fixed chain.

## Unavoidable stack upgrade (and why)
Gemini **3.8 is a "thinking" model** and requires a `thought_signature` on tool calls across turns. The old `langchain-google-genai` 2.0.7 couldn't round-trip it, so the agent died on turn 2. Fixing it meant upgrading to `langchain-google-genai 4.4.1` → which pulled `langchain-core 1.6`, `langgraph 1.2`, `pydantic 2.14`, `mcp 2.3`, `fastapi 0.143`. I adapted `mcp_client.py` to the mcp 2.x API (2-tuple transport, token-in-URL auth, Markdown search/details with a `fetch_input_schema` that parses the JSON block; the run path stays JSON). All pinned in `requirements.txt`.

## Verified (live HTTP)
Autonomous run: **4 searches + 3 inspections, then the agent self-submitted** → Google Maps Reviews Scraper with evidence (4.85/5, 61k users, $0.0006/review, date filter) and honest alternatives → form from the real schema → `/run` returned **20 real review cards** (and `maxReviews` capped). I also saw the fallback engage cleanly earlier when the budget was hit — proving the guardrail.

Both servers are live (app **http://localhost:5173**). README updated with the new architecture, the guardrails, and the upgrade rationale.

One caveat worth flagging: if you tighten `AGENT_MAX_STEPS` too far, a thorough agent run will fall back to the deterministic pick (thinner reasoning) rather than its own — 12 is a comfortable default. Want me to surface guardrail events (e.g. "research budget reached") in the UI progress stream so that behavior is visible during the demo?

Good question — let me clear this up, because both things are true at once.

**Yes, the agent absolutely uses Apify MCP.** When the agent calls its `search_sources` / `get_source_details` tools, those execute against the Apify **MCP** server. The chain is:

```
agent (Gemini)
  └─ calls tool "search_sources"      ← LangChain tool bound to Gemini
       └─ tools_node executes it
            └─ mcp_client.search_actors(...)
                 └─ Apify MCP tool  "search-actors"      ← real MCP call
  └─ calls tool "get_source_details"
       └─ mcp_client.fetch_actor_details(...)
            └─ Apify MCP tool  "fetch-actor-details"     ← real MCP call
```

So discovery is 100% MCP. What I meant by **"no run tool"** is about *which* MCP tools the agent is allowed to reach.

The Apify MCP server also exposes `call-actor` (and `get-dataset-items`) — the tools that **actually run a scraper and cost money**. I deliberately do **not** give the agent a wrapper for those. Its whitelist is only search + details. So:

- **Agent (autonomous)** → MCP `search-actors`, `fetch-actor-details` (read-only, cheap).
- **`/run` endpoint (explicit user click, capped)** → `mcp_client.call_actor` → MCP `call-actor` + `get-dataset-items`.

Both go through MCP. The guardrail is that the *autonomous* loop can't reach the *expensive* MCP tool — only a deliberate user action can.

**Why wrap the MCP tools instead of binding the raw Apify MCP tool list to Gemini?** Two reasons:
1. **Control the whitelist** — if I bound Apify's whole MCP toolset directly, the LLM would also get `call-actor`, `abort-actor-run`, `get-key-value-store-record`, etc., and could run scrapers on its own. Wrapping lets me expose exactly three capabilities.
2. **Apply the guardrails per call** — budgets/counters, retries, timeouts, and id-validation live in `tools_node`.

If you'd prefer the more "MCP-native" style — bind the actual Apify MCP tools to the agent and filter the list to exclude `call-actor` — I can switch to that (e.g. via `langchain-mcp-adapters`), keeping the same whitelist guardrail. The current wrapper approach just gives tighter control. Want me to make that change?

Fixed and measured. Search wasn't hanging — individual calls are fast (~5s); the time was the agent doing *many* searches. Now it's capped.

**After the change** (just measured, live):
```
 2.2s  Searching for "google maps reviews"…
 6.6s  Searched "google maps reviews" (5 found)   ← ONE search, 5 results, then stops
10–27s inspected 3 candidates
39s    Choosing the best fit…
50s    Built the form
TOTAL recommend: ~50s
```

What I changed:
- **5 results per search** (`SEARCH_RESULTS_PER_QUERY=5`) — hard-clamped server-side, the agent can't override it.
- **Stops searching early** — `AGENT_MAX_SEARCHES=2` (prompt tells it to do **one** search and only search again if the first clearly misses), then move straight to deciding. In the run above it did exactly one.

So the "search" part is now ~4s and done. The remaining ~44s is **not search** — it's 3 source inspections (~18s), the final reasoning turn (~12s), and building the form (~11s). Those are Gemini/MCP calls, not searching.

If 50s total still feels too long, the biggest lever is **inspections** — drop `MAX_CANDIDATES_TO_INSPECT` from 6 to 2. That would cut ~12s. Want me to set that too, or leave it so the agent can still compare a few sources properly?

All knobs live in `backend/.env` (no code change needed to tune):
```
SEARCH_RESULTS_PER_QUERY=5
AGENT_MAX_SEARCHES=2
MAX_CANDIDATES_TO_INSPECT=6
```

That confirms it: the Vite proxy **does** stream SSE live (progress events arrived incrementally). The only problem was the browser-side parser splitting on the wrong line-ending — now fixed.

**What to do:** hard-refresh the app in your browser (**Ctrl+Shift+R**) to pick up the hot-reloaded `api.js`, then run a search again. You'll now see the live progress steps and the recommendation.

Summary of the bug/fix:
- Backend + proxy were fine (verified streaming through `localhost:5173/api`).
- `frontend/src/api.js` split the stream on `\n\n`, but the server separates SSE frames with `\r\n\r\n`, so no frames ever parsed and the UI sat on the "searching" screen forever.
- Fix: normalize `\r\n` → `\n` before splitting. Vite HMR already reloaded it.

Give it a try now. If it still looks stuck after a hard refresh, open the browser devtools **Console/Network** tab and tell me what you see on the `recommend` request — but this was the cause.

207	async def _poll_run(session: ClientSession, run_id: str, deadline_secs: int) -> dict:
208	    """Wait for a run to reach a terminal state, within an overall budget."""
209	    import time as _time
210	
211	    tool = await _resolve_tool(session, "get-actor-run")
212	    start = _time.monotonic()
213	    last: dict = {}
214	    while _time.monotonic() - start < deadline_secs:
215	        remaining = deadline_secs - (_time.monotonic() - start)
216	        wait = max(1, min(int(remaining), 30))

219	            res = next((p for p in res if isinstance(p, dict)), {})
220	        last = res or last
221	        if (last.get("status") or "").upper() in _TERMINAL:
222	            break
223	    return last

## Page contract — read before your first publish

These are the Artifact tool's own rules for the file you publish; the design guidance below builds on them.

**Format**: Always author the page as `.html`. Publish a `.md` file only when a loaded skill explicitly instructs it. When the user shares a markdown document or asks to turn one into an artifact, author an HTML page based on its content — preserve its substance, and design the page as you would any other artifact rather than transcribing the markdown one-to-one.

**Skeleton**: The file is wrapped in a `<!doctype html>…<head>…</head><body>` skeleton at publish time, so write the page content directly — no `<!DOCTYPE>`, `<html>`, `<head>`, or `<body>` tags of your own. Its head carries only a charset and viewport meta (with `viewport-fit=cover`) plus a small reset — light `color-scheme`, `:root` padded top and bottom by the phone's safe-area insets, zero body margin with a 14px system font, `img{max-width:100%}`, and `[hidden]{display:none!important}` (toggle visibility with `el.hidden`, not `style.display`) — so put your own `<title>` and `<style>` at the top of the file. Keep the `:root` padding: a bar fixed to the top or bottom stays at `0` and adds `env(safe-area-inset-top, 0px)` or `env(safe-area-inset-bottom, 0px)` to its own padding, and a sticky page header uses `top: env(safe-area-inset-top, 0px)`, not `0`.

**Title**: Put a `<title>` at the top of the HTML; only the first 8KB of the file is scanned for it. It is the artifact's name in the browser tab and the gallery, so write a name, not a summary: a short noun phrase, typically two to four words, specific enough to pick this page out among many, the way an app or a document is named. When the user already has a specific name for the thing, use that name for the title rather than coining a new one. Never use a generic category label alone, and never append an explainer after a dash or colon. If you shorten a title that pairs the name with a generic word, keep the name, not the generic word. A multi-word title that already reads as one specific name is finished; do not shorten it further. The explanation goes in the one-sentence `description` parameter, which becomes the gallery card's subtitle. The `title` parameter fills in only when an HTML file has no `<title>` tag (Markdown pages keep their filename). Keep the title stable across redeploys.

**External resources — CDN allowlist (CSP-enforced)**: external scripts load ONLY from https://cdnjs.cloudflare.com (preferred), https://cdn.jsdelivr.net/npm/, https://unpkg.com, https://cdn.tailwindcss.com (Tailwind's play-CDN script) and https://code.jquery.com; external stylesheets ONLY from https://fonts.googleapis.com, with the font files they pull from https://fonts.gstatic.com (give every face a real fallback stack). Everything else is blocked, with no visible error: every other host (esm.sh included) and, even on those CDNs, anything but a script — stylesheets, images, media, fetch/XHR/WebSocket, a library's runtime fetches. So inline all other CSS and JS and embed assets as data: URIs. **How to load a library**: `<script src="https://cdnjs.cloudflare.com/ajax/libs/<lib>/<exact version>/<file>">` — pick the UMD build, which defines a global (e.g. react/18.3.1/umd/react.production.min.js, then react-dom) — placed BEFORE any inline `<script>` that uses it. Pin every library URL to an exact version (`react@18.3.1` on jsDelivr or unpkg, `react/18.3.1/…` on cdnjs, `cdn.tailwindcss.com/3.4.17`), never a bare name, a range or a tag (`react`, `react@18`, `@latest`). Choose a version at least two weeks old. Any version you knew before this conversation is old enough; if you look versions up, skip any published in the last two weeks. The viewer's sandbox also blocks any download the page starts itself — `<a download>` links (data:/blob: hrefs included) and script-driven saves are inert for viewers — so never offer a file through a plain link. Links to other websites (`https://…`) open in a new tab, but email, phone and app links (`mailto:`, `tel:`, `sms:`, other custom schemes) are unreliable inside an artifact: for many viewers (for example anyone outside the user's organization, or anyone viewing through a public link) following one, by link or by script, often does not work, and the page cannot tell whether it did. So show the address or number itself as selectable text (a copy button helps), treat such a link as a convenience that may do nothing, and never tell the viewer a message was sent or a call placed because the viewer tapped one. Artifacts render mermaid diagrams natively — markdown via ```mermaid fences, HTML via `<pre class="mermaid">` blocks — no library needed, don't load one. The viewer never shows `alert()`, `confirm()` or `prompt()` dialogs — `confirm()` returns false and `prompt()` returns null immediately — so build any confirmation step into the page itself.

**What the viewer's frame allows**: The page runs in a locked-down frame; what it refuses below, it refuses for every viewer (anonymous, signed-in, embedded, desktop and mobile apps), so build around these limits instead of detecting them. The page cannot open the print dialog — `window.print()` does nothing — so never offer a Print or "Save as PDF" button. Forms work as page UI (inputs, validation, submit events), but a real submission has nowhere to go: handle `submit` in script with `preventDefault()` and never point `action` at another site or a mailto: address. Copy buttons work when `navigator.clipboard.writeText` is called inside the click handler — catch its rejection (older desktop apps and some app views refuse it) and fall back to selecting the text; reading the clipboard never works, though the viewer's own Paste (the `paste` event) does. Camera, microphone, screen capture, location, motion, Web Share and similar device APIs are refused without a prompt — build on one only when this user has a runtime capability for that device (the `artifact-capabilities` skill lists them) and the page declares it; otherwise take photos, audio and data as uploaded files instead (file inputs, drag-and-drop of files and `FileReader` work in browsers). A screen wake lock may be granted while the page is visible: request it and tolerate rejection. Fullscreen and pointer lock work from a click in desktop browsers; treat both as optional (handle the rejection) since phones and some app views lack them. Sound plays only after the viewer interacts (muted autoplay is fine), so start audio from a button. Other sites cannot be embedded — no YouTube, map or form iframes, and no `<object>`/`<embed>`; link out instead: links open outside the artifact, normally in a new tab, while `window.open` works only for some signed-in viewers in the artifact's own organization and returns null for everyone else, so use real `<a href>` links. Web Workers work from your own files or `blob:` URLs; service workers and WebRTC do not. `fetch()` of files published alongside the page works with relative URLs; images from your own files, `data:` or `blob:` URLs draw to canvas and export cleanly. Only a plain `#anchor` (letters, digits, `.` `_` `~` `-`) from the artifact's link reaches `location.hash` — never `#key=value` state and never the query string — so deep-link to a tab or section with a bare token and keep all other state in the page.

**Browser storage**: `localStorage` (also `sessionStorage` and IndexedDB) works, but each artifact has its own origin and the data lives only in that viewer's browser — it survives republishes to the same URL and never reaches other viewers, other devices, or Claude. It can come back empty or the accessor can throw (a private window, cleared or blocked site data, previews or thumbnail capture), so wrap every read and write in try/catch and render the page correctly without it. Use it only for per-viewer conveniences (a remembered tab or filter, a collapsed section, an unsent draft), never for state that must persist reliably, be shared between viewers, or be read back by Claude — state like that belongs in a runtime capability when this user has one: load the `artifact-capabilities` skill before writing the page.

**Size**: The rendered page must be 16MB or smaller, and embedded data: URIs count toward that.

**Responsive**: The page must also work at phone width (about 400px), and the page body must never scroll horizontally. Keep a side gutter of at least 16px at every width: set it once as side padding on `body` or one outer wrapper, and give that element its vertical padding with `padding-block`, never a `padding` shorthand that zeroes the sides. Use relative units. Let flex and grid rows wrap or stack to one column when narrow, and give any flex or grid child that holds running text, code or a table `min-width: 0`, so long content wraps or scrolls inside it instead of pushing the page wider. Put `max-width: 100%` on images and on any `aspect-ratio` box, and give nothing a `min-width` wider than the screen. Only tables, diagrams and code blocks may be wider, each inside its own `overflow-x: auto` container.

**Theme-aware**: The page renders in the viewer's theme, which has three states: an explicit choice sets `data-theme="dark"` or `data-theme="light"` on the root element, and the default "system" setting sets nothing, so for most viewers only `prefers-color-scheme` tells light from dark. Define every color as a token, in this shape (token names and count are the design's own):
```css
:root { --bg: …; --fg: …; --accent: … }  /* every token, light values */
@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark) { :root:not([data-theme="light"]) { --bg: …; --fg: …; --accent: …; color-scheme: dark } }
:root[data-theme="dark"] { --bg: …; --fg: …; --accent: …; color-scheme: dark }  /* same dark values, so the toggle wins both ways */
body { background: var(--bg); color: var(--fg) }
```
Every token gets its first definition on bare `:root`; the two dark blocks only redefine tokens, and their `color-scheme: dark` makes form controls and scrollbars follow. No color has its only definition inside a media or `[data-theme]` block, and no component rule uses a literal color that reads in one theme only. `body` keeps that explicit token background: the viewer paints its own ground behind the page, so a transparent body shows the host's theme instead. A dark-first design mirrors the whole shape, selectors included: dark values and `color-scheme: dark` on bare `:root` (the skeleton pins `light` there), light values and `color-scheme: light` under `(prefers-color-scheme: light)` guarded `:root:not([data-theme="dark"])` and again under `:root[data-theme="light"]`. A design that deliberately commits to a single look may drop the two dark blocks but still sets the background and every color explicitly, plus `color-scheme: dark` on `:root` if that look is dark.

**Icon** (on every first publish): Pass one short generic word as `icon` (e.g. `"chart"`, `"calendar"`, `"recipe"`) for the artifact's browser-tab icon — a plain signifier for what the page is, never a product or brand name, and never an emoji or markup. It stays the **same** for the life of an artifact, so on a redeploy (the same file path this session, or `url`) omit `icon` and the artifact keeps the one it has; pass a different one only when the user asks.

Work the way the design lead at a small, versatile studio would: give each client a visual identity at the level of treatment the task calls for. Make deliberate choices about palette, typography, and layout that are specific to this subject, and avoid templated designs.

## Read the request first

Decide the treatment; designing is a given. A doc gets the same craft as a landing page; only the treatment differs. Format is a separate matter: author HTML, and publish Markdown only when a loaded skill explicitly instructs it. A Markdown publish keeps its filename as its title, uses almost none of the craft below, and is never a way to save time.

Many requests call for a more utilitarian treatment: a plan, a memo, a demo. Make it polished, with real typographic hierarchy, considered spacing, and a proper palette, but avoid over-designing. Most pages don't need a flashy, gigantic hero. Keep flourishes tasteful and limited.

Some requests call for an editorial treatment: a landing page, a game, an app or tool they'll keep or share.

If unsure: a well-composed page is always acceptable; an over-designed visual identity sometimes isn't.

Fundamentals below apply to everything. Follow the editorial process after them only when that reading calls for it.

## Fundamentals for every artifact

**Respect what already exists.** Look for an existing design system first: CLAUDE.md, a tokens or theme file, existing component styles. When one exists, apply it; everything below fills gaps and never overrides. Precedence is always the user's own words, then the project's existing system, then your choices.

**Ground it in the subject.** If the subject isn't already clear, define it: one concrete subject, its audience, and the page's single job. Distinctive choices come from the subject's own world: its materials, instruments, and vernacular. Whatever the treatment, include at least one detail only this subject would have (its real units and scales, its document conventions, its terms of art) as content rather than ornament; it costs nothing even on a plain page. Use real content throughout and never lorem ipsum.

**Pair typefaces.** Typography determines how the page reads even when the page isn't about typography. Google Fonts is the only font host the Artifact CSP allows; link it directly (`<link rel="stylesheet" href="https://fonts.googleapis.com/css2?family=...&display=swap">`). A face from anywhere else must be inlined as a @font-face data URI, or the browser silently uses a fallback. In both cases, declare a real fallback stack. Keep running text near 65 characters wide. Set a type scale and keep to it. Give headings `text-wrap: balance`, give body text comfortable spacing, and give uppercase labels a little letter-spacing.

**Load libraries instead of inlining them.** When the page really needs a library (React, a charting or highlighting package), load its UMD build from cdnjs with one `<script src="https://cdnjs.cloudflare.com/ajax/libs/...">`, pinned to an exact version at least two weeks old and placed before the inline script that uses its global; don't inline the library's source or hand-write a substitute. Only the script loads this way; a library's stylesheet still has to be inlined, and the page contract above lists the few other script hosts the CSP admits. The page's own CSS and JS, its images, and its data ship with the page. Most pages don't need any library; use one only when it does substantial work for the page.

**Choose neutrals deliberately.** A pure mid-grey looks unconsidered; a grey with a slight hue bias toward the page's accent looks intentional. Pure white and near-black are fine backgrounds when they suit the subject, as long as you chose the neutral deliberately instead of inheriting a default.

**Design both themes.** The page renders in the viewer's theme, and the viewer has three states: an explicit choice sets `data-theme="dark"` or `data-theme="light"` on the root element, and the default "system" setting sets *nothing*. Most viewers see the document with no `data-theme` attribute, where only `prefers-color-scheme` distinguishes light from dark. Structure the CSS at the token level for all three. The bare `:root` block defines the complete light palette (for a deliberately dark-first design, swap light and dark consistently through this whole pattern); `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)` redefines only the tokens, guarded as `:root:not([data-theme="light"])` so an explicit light choice overrides a dark OS setting; `:root[data-theme="dark"]` redefines them again so the toggle also overrides in the other direction; wherever the dark palette applies - both dark blocks, or bare `:root` in a dark-first or single-dark design - also set `color-scheme: dark` (the skeleton pins `light` on `:root`), so native form controls and scrollbars follow the palette. Style components through the tokens, never directly inside a media or `[data-theme]` block: a color defined only inside `[data-theme]` never applies when no `data-theme` attribute is set, and the page then shows one theme's text on the other theme's background. Two more rules keep each theme consistent. First, the artifact is composited over a background the viewer paints in *its own* theme, so `body` must set an explicit `background` from a token; a transparent body shows the host's background with no warning. Second, every element that sets a color takes it from the same token set as the surface behind it, never from a literal that works in only one theme. Declare every token in the bare `:root` block before any media or `[data-theme]` block redefines it; a color that exists only inside one of those blocks is the classic unreadable-artifact bug. Give the second theme the same care as the first: don't simply invert it; keep contrast legible and keep the accent working on both backgrounds. A design that deliberately commits to one visual world (a neon arcade screen, a letterpress invitation) may stay single-theme: then omit the media query and `[data-theme]` blocks entirely but still set the background and every color explicitly, so the page looks right on either host background; do this by choice, never by omission.

**Use layout for spacing.** Lay out sibling groups with flex or grid and `gap` instead of per-element margins, which collapse or double without warning. Keep a side gutter of at least 16px at every width: set it once as side padding on `body` or one outer wrapper, whose vertical padding uses `padding-block` and never a `padding` shorthand that zeroes the sides. Let rows wrap or stack to one column at phone width (about 400px). Give images and any `aspect-ratio` box `max-width: 100%`, and don't give anything a `min-width` wider than the screen. Only wide tables, code, and diagrams may exceed it; give each `overflow-x: auto` on its own container so the page body never scrolls sideways. The publish skeleton pads `:root` top and bottom by the phone's safe-area insets (zero everywhere except a phone app) so the page runs edge to edge while its content stays clear of the system bars; keep that padding. A bar fixed to the top or bottom stays at `0` and adds `env(safe-area-inset-top, 0px)` or `env(safe-area-inset-bottom, 0px)` to its own padding. A sticky page header uses `top: env(safe-area-inset-top, 0px)` and never `0`. Size a one-screen app with `height: 100%` on `html` and `body` instead of `100vh`, so it fits inside that padding. A page that includes its own viewport meta gets this padding only when that meta declares `viewport-fit=cover`. Use `font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums` wherever digits line up in columns.

**Make repeated elements consistent.** For cards in a row, label/value pairs down a list, or badges on sibling items, use the same edges, baselines, and inner padding on each, and put any recurring element in the same place on each. Let content set a container's height and pick a column count the items fill, so nothing stretches over empty space or sits alone in a row. Make text that can outgrow its track wrap or scroll in its own container; clipped text is a bug.

**Use card styling selectively.** Border, fill, radius, and shadow each mark an element as a separate object. Apply them by role, to set off the one element that needs it; applying the same radius and shadow to every block flattens the hierarchy. Open with big-number tiles only when those figures are the point of the page.

**Draw charts to scale.** Place marks, ticks, and labels with one scale, and make every label name a value the chart actually reaches. Color chart text from the theme tokens so it is readable in both themes. Keep marks, labels, and edges clear of one another and inside the drawing's bounds; in SVG, leave room in the viewBox for the outermost labels and give every drawn shape an explicit fill.

**Make the page complete at rest.** Everything meant to be read is visible once the page has loaded, with no scrolling to trigger it; that first still frame is what a thumbnail, a shared link, and a skimming reader all see. A section may animate in, but from a visible resting state, never left at `opacity: 0` waiting for an observer. Size a hero to its content instead of to the viewport; a `100vh` opener pushes the rest of the page out of that first frame. A tool or app opens in a realistic working state: the user's real data where it exists, otherwise example rows, a loaded sample, or a plausibly filled form, clearly marked as examples and never presented as the user's own figures. The first view shows what the tool does; an empty shell waiting for input shows nothing. When those records live in the `db` capability, keep them out of the page source. Its first frame renders before the store answers, so make that frame a designed empty state that names what will appear and how to add the first entry, then fill it from the store. Seed the store through the `ArtifactData` tool (what type instructions call `write_db`) after the first publish: the user's real data, or marked examples for a tool only the user will use. For a store other people will fill with their own entries, seed only what the user gave you or asked for, never invented people or records.

**Avoid AI-generated design.** AI-generated design currently clusters around a few looks: warm cream (#F4F1EA) with a serif display and terracotta accent; near-black with a lone acid-green or vermilion pop; broadsheet hairline rules with dense columns; a purple-to-blue gradient hero on white; Inter or Space Grotesk as the "safe" face; emoji as section markers; everything centered; `rounded-lg` everywhere; accent bar/rail on rounded cards. When the user specifies a visual direction, follow it exactly; their words always win, including when they ask for one of these looks. When nothing is specified, don't use that freedom on one of these defaults.

**Build cleanly.** Watch for overlapping elements, cascade collisions, and silent font fallbacks. Close every non-void element, double-quote attributes, give keyboard focus a visible state, and respect `prefers-reduced-motion`. Give every form control a stable `id` (the platform preserves form values, focus, and scroll position across a republish). For generative or decorative graphics, use Canvas or WebGL instead of hand-writing long SVG path data.

**CSS rules.** When writing the CSS, watch your selector specificity. It is easy to generate classes that cancel each other out, e.g. a type-based selector like `.section` and an element-based one like `.cta` both setting padding and margins between sections. Structure the cascade so it doesn't undo your spacing unnoticed.

**Writing the copy.** Treat words as design material and never as decoration. Write from the user's side of the screen: name things by what people recognize instead of how the system is built (a person manages *notifications*; they don't manage *webhook config*). Use active voice; a control states exactly what happens ("Publish", then a toast that says "Published"). Errors explain what went wrong and how to fix it, without apologies or vagueness. Prefer specific to clever. Write plainly, the way a knowledgeable person would talk. Avoid mannered devices: asides set off by em-dashes, "not X, but Y" framing, colon-then-reveal sentences, scare quotes around invented labels, and stock phrases such as "worth noting" or "honest caveat". Prefer short, direct sentences over compressed or clever phrasing.

**Name the page like a product; don't caption it.** The `<title>` is the artifact's name in the gallery and the browser tab, and it gives the reader a first impression of the care taken. Give the page a real name: a short noun phrase, typically two to four words, specific to the subject; or, for a page that exists to answer one question, that question itself, which then is the page's name. When the user already has a specific name for the thing, use that name for the title rather than coining a new one. Stop at the name; a title that adds its own explanation after a dash or colon reads as generated filler. The name must also identify the page among many: in the gallery it appears beside dozens of other artifacts, and a generic category label that could apply to any of them fails as a name just as an appended explanation does. When a candidate title combines the name with a generic word (a greeting, a category, a page-type label), keep the name; a trim that drops the identifying part and keeps the generic word produces a title that could apply to any page. This rule removes explanations and doesn't require brevity: a multi-word title that already reads as one specific name is finished, and shortening it further only makes it generic. Put the explanation in the one-sentence publish `description`; the gallery shows it directly under the title.

**Structure is information.** Structural devices (numbering, eyebrows, dividers, labels) should encode something true about the content instead of decorating it. Many generic designs use numbered markers (01 / 02 / 03), but those fit only if the content actually is a sequence, such as a real process or a typed timeline where the order is information the reader needs. Before adding numbered markers or similar devices, check that they actually make sense.

**When the page is a UI (dashboard, tool).** It is scanned and operated instead of read top to bottom, so the craft shifts from typography to information design. Put the summary before the detail. Encode state in form as well as in numbers (a pill, a chip, a severity stripe) so that whatever needs attention is visible at a glance. Semantic color (good / warning / critical) is separate from the accent hue and doesn't count as your accent. Give sparklines and charts the same care as type: an area fill, a faint grid, an emphasized endpoint. Interactive elements should look interactive.



## Process

Start from what the viewer should be able to do on the page, in addition to what they will read. If the page should take input, keep what people change for whoever opens it next, show live data, or ask Claude something, load the `artifact-capabilities` skill now and design around what it makes available to this user. A page that is only read doesn't need that.

Before writing the page, settle a short design plan (a compact token system) and write it into the file itself, as the `:root` block at the top of the page's `<style>`, rather than into your reply:
- **Color**: the palette as 4-6 named color tokens.
- **Type**: font tokens for 2+ roles: a characterful display face used with restraint, a complementary body face, and a utility face for captions or data if needed.
- **Layout**: the layout concept as a one-line comment above the tokens.

Then build the rest of the page from those tokens, deriving every color and type decision from them. The plan is working material, not part of the answer: unless the user asks about the design, one plain sentence on the direction is the most to say about it, with no hex values or font names.

**Write, check once, publish.** Before publishing you may look at the rendered page once, where this session offers a way: one `ArtifactCheck` preview (or the Artifact tool's own `action: "preview"` where there is no separate `ArtifactCheck` tool), or else one screenshot of the local file; if the session offers none of these, skip the look. The preview renders desktop and phone widths in light and dark and lists overflow, colors that ignore the theme, blocked loads and console errors, including a script that fails to parse, so nothing it covers needs a check of your own. The look is optional: if you take it, make one pass of edits for what it shows, without a second look; then publish. For a page that charts real numbers, take the look rather than skip it, and spend it on the chart. A page whose point is logic (dates, money, scoring, parsing) may get one more check before publishing: one run of a pure function on a sample input, or, where no preview ran, one syntax check of its script; nothing more. A page that declares `capabilities` takes the look rather than skipping it, then one functional pass after the first publish, because the preview cannot run that code: read back once what the page stores or serves (one read of its stored data or one read-only route call; where that read alone would prove nothing, as on an empty store, you may first write one probe document yourself and delete it after the read, passing the `version` the write returned as `if_version`; no other test writes unless the user wants one), tell the user in one line what you exercised and what you could not, and stop. None of this becomes a loop, because the user is waiting for a link: no second screenshot, no scripts that probe the DOM, no re-running a check that passed. Review happens on the live page, and further polish is for the user to request; if the user reports something visibly broken (a clipped column, unreadable text, a control that does nothing), fix that, take at most one more look if the session offers a way, and republish once. These checks are for code you wrote into this page, not for content you fill into an Artifact made from an Artifact type (a Slides deck, a Design canvas): there the type's own instructions say whether to check, and if they say nothing, don't.

**Open viewers.** You don't need to do anything for viewers who already have the page open: published changes are delivered to them automatically at their next quiet moment, with state preserved where possible. If your page has state a viewer would miss (a game, a long form), register `window.claude?.hot?.snapshot(...)` and boot through `window.claude?.hot?.ready ? window.claude.hot.ready(start) : start(window.claude?.hot?.data ?? {})`; `hot` is the viewer's own update hook, not a capability behind `claude.use()`, and is absent outside a viewer, hence the `?.`.

## When the request is editorial

The stance changes here: the client has already rejected proposals that felt templated, and is paying for a distinctive point of view. Make opinionated decisions, and take one real aesthetic risk where it serves the work.

Review the design plan against the subject before building: if any part of it reads like the generic default you would produce for any similar page, revise that part in the plan itself, without narrating the revision to the user. Write the code only after the plan is specific to this subject, following the revised plan exactly.

**Principles** 

- The hero states the thesis: open with the most characteristic thing in the subject's world (headline, image, live demo, interactive moment). 
- Typography sets the personality of the page. Pair the display and body faces deliberately, avoiding the families you would use on any other project, and set a clear type scale with intentional weights, widths, and spacing. Make the type treatment itself a memorable part of the design instead of a neutral container for the content. 
- Use motion deliberately. Think about whether and where animation can serve the subject: a page-load sequence, hover micro-interactions, ambient atmosphere. One orchestrated moment is usually more effective than scattered effects; choose what the direction calls for. However, sometimes less is more, and extra animation adds to the impression that the design is AI-generated. 
- Match complexity to the vision. Maximalist directions need elaborate execution; minimal directions need precision in spacing, type, and detail. Elegance is executing the chosen vision well.
- Put your boldness in one place; keep everything around it quiet. If the accent clashes with the background, shift it toward an analogous hue or desaturate it instead of replacing it.

45	How to judge:
46	- Default to NOT asking. If the request is already specific enough to pick a source, set
47	  needs_clarification=false and ask nothing. Do not invent questions to have some.
48	- Only set true when a real ambiguity would change the chosen source or the run.
49	
50	Hard rules:
51	- NEVER ask something you can reasonably infer. Do not ask for technical details.
52	- No jargon. Never say API, scraper, dataset, actor, endpoint, or schema.
53	- Plain words a marketer uses. Each question must be answerable in a few seconds.
54	- Prefer "choice" questions with 2-5 concrete options when the dimension is well-known
55	  (e.g. region, freshness). Use "text" only when options can't be enumerated.
56	- Every choice question MUST include options. Always allow a custom answer.
57	- Phrase options as human labels (value can equal the label)."""
58	
59	
60	def _clarify_fallback(need: str) -> _ClarifyOut:
61	    # Reliability fallback: if the model can't be reached, skip clarifying rather than fail —
62	    # the discovery step can still run on the raw need.
63	    return _ClarifyOut(restated_need=need, questions=[])
64	
65	
66	async def generate_clarifying_questions(need: str) -> _ClarifyOut:
67	    out = await structured(
68	        _ClarifyOut,
69	        system=SYSTEM,
70	        user=f'The user typed: "{need}"',
71	        temperature=0.3,
72	        label="clarify",
73	        validate=lambda o: ([] if (o.restated_need or "").strip() else ["missing restated need"]),
74	        fallback=lambda: _clarify_fallback(need),
75	    )
76	    # Safety net: cap questions and guarantee IDs/options are well-formed.
77	    cleaned: list[ClarifyQuestion] = []
78	    for i, q in enumerate(out.questions[:4]):
79	        if not q.id:
80	            q.id = f"q{i+1}"
81	        if q.kind == "choice" and not q.options:
82	            q.kind = "text"
83	        # de-dup empty option labels
84	        q.options = [ClarifyOption(value=o.value or o.label, label=o.label or o.value)
85	                     for o in q.options if (o.label or o.value)]
86	        cleaned.append(q)
87	    out.questions = cleaned
88	    return out

Done. Clarifying is now an explicit **LLM-as-judge** decision.

**Code change** (`backend/app/agent/clarify.py`):
- The model now returns a `needs_clarification` boolean (its judgment) plus a one-line `judge_reason`, before any questions.
- The judge **defaults to not asking** — it only sets `true` when a real ambiguity would change which source wins or how it's run and it can't infer the answer.
- The verdict is **hard-enforced**: if the judge says no, questions are dropped to `[]` and the app goes straight to search. The reliability fallback also defaults to skip.

**Verified live (just now):**
- `cool recipes` → judged *needs clarification* → 2 questions (where from / what kind).
- `the 20 newest Google Maps reviews from the last 30 days for coffee shops in Chicago, newest first` → judged *skip* → **0 questions**, straight to search.

1	"""Deterministic validators — pure functions, no LLM, no network.
2	
3	Two jobs:
4	  1. Validate *input* data at the API boundary (the need text, the run form values) before we
5	     spend any LLM or Apify call on it.
6	  2. Validate *output* data from each LLM step (queries, shortlist, recommendation, form,
7	     display) so a malformed or hallucinated result is caught and retried, and never reaches
8	     the user or a downstream Apify call.
9	
10	Each validator returns a list of human-readable error strings; an empty list means valid.
11	Keeping these deterministic (and cheap) is what lets us retry only when necessary and fall
12	back without another model call.
13	"""
14	from __future__ import annotations
15	
16	from typing import Any
17	
18	MAX_NEED_LEN = 500
19	MIN_NEED_LEN = 3
20	MAX_QUERY_LEN = 120
21	
22	
23	# --------------------------- input validation ---------------------------
24	def clean_need(need: str) -> str:
25	    return " ".join((need or "").split()).strip()
26	
27	
28	def validate_need(need: str) -> list[str]:
29	    errors: list[str] = []
30	    n = clean_need(need)
31	    if len(n) < MIN_NEED_LEN:
32	        errors.append("Please describe what data you're looking for.")
33	    if len(n) > MAX_NEED_LEN:
34	        errors.append(f"That's a bit long — please keep it under {MAX_NEED_LEN} characters.")
35	    return errors
36	
37	
38	def schema_props(detail: dict) -> dict:
39	    return (detail.get("inputSchema") or {}).get("properties") or {}
40	
41	
42	def required_keys(detail: dict) -> set[str]:
43	    return set((detail.get("inputSchema") or {}).get("required") or [])
44	
45	
46	def validate_run_input(values: dict, detail: dict) -> list[str]:
47	    """Check required inputs are present before running a source. Returns the schema keys
48	    that are missing (caller maps them to friendly labels)."""
49	    props = schema_props(detail)
50	    required = required_keys(detail)
51	    missing: list[str] = []
52	    for key in required:
53	        if key not in props:
54	            continue
55	        val = values.get(key)
56	        if val in (None, "", [], {}):
57	            missing.append(key)
58	    return missing
59	
60	
61	# --------------------------- output validation ---------------------------
62	def validate_plan(out: Any) -> list[str]:
63	    errors: list[str] = []
64	    queries = [q for q in (getattr(out, "queries", None) or []) if str(q).strip()]
65	    if not queries:
66	        errors.append("no search queries produced")
67	    if any(len(str(q)) > MAX_QUERY_LEN for q in queries):
68	        errors.append("a query is too long")
69	    if not str(getattr(out, "restated_need", "") or "").strip():
70	        errors.append("missing restated need")
71	    return errors
72	
73	
74	def validate_shortlist(shortlist: list[str], valid_ids: set[str]) -> list[str]:
75	    valid = [s for s in (shortlist or []) if s in valid_ids]
76	    if not valid:
77	        return ["shortlist has no valid source ids"]
78	    return [

Here's exactly which deterministic validators are in use (all pure functions — no LLM, no network).

## Active validators

**Input guards** — run before spending any LLM/Apify call:

| Validator | File | Checks |
|---|---|---|
| `clean_need` + `validate_need` | [validation.py:24-35](backend/app/validation.py#L24-L35) → wired at [main.py:50](backend/app/main.py#L50) | need is non-empty, ≤ 500 chars |
| `validate_run_input` | [validation.py:46-58](backend/app/validation.py#L46-L58) → wired at [main.py:195](backend/app/main.py#L195) | every **required** input key is present before a run (returns the missing keys → friendly labels) |

**Output guards** — validate what the LLM produced; a failure triggers a retry, then fallback:

| Validator | File | Checks |
|---|---|---|
| `_validate_submit` (the agent's terminal action) | [discovery.py](backend/app/agent/discovery.py) | chosen `source_id` is a **real** id the agent actually saw; `name`/`headline`/`reasons` present; a "no match" must carry a `message` |
| clarify judge check (inline) | [clarify.py:73](backend/app/agent/clarify.py#L73) | `restated_need` present |
| `validate_form` | [validation.py:101-109](backend/app/validation.py#L101-L109) → [form.py:171](backend/app/agent/form.py#L171) | ≥ 1 form field is **bound to a real input key** in the schema |
| `validate_display` | [validation.py:112-122](backend/app/validation.py#L112-L122) → [display.py:83](backend/app/agent/display.py#L83) | has a summary; any named card field (title/image/link/rating) **exists in the real data** |
| `sanitize_items` | [validation.py:125-129](backend/app/validation.py#L125-L129) → [main.py:211](backend/app/main.py#L211) | result rows are kept only if well-formed dicts |

## Honest note — 3 dead ones

`validate_plan`, `validate_shortlist`, and `validate_recommendation` (validation.py:62-98) were for the **old fixed pipeline** (plan → triage → decide). When we switched to the autonomous agent, its own `_validate_submit` in `discovery.py` replaced `validate_recommendation`, and plan/shortlist stages no longer exist — so those three are now unused.

Want me to **remove the 3 dead validators** (and move `_validate_submit` into `validation.py` so every deterministic validator lives in one place)? That'd make the module match what's actually running.

1	"""Deterministic validators — pure functions, no LLM, no network.
2	
3	Two jobs:
4	  1. Validate *input* data at the API boundary (the need text, the run form values) before we
5	     spend any LLM or Apify call on it.
6	  2. Validate *output* data from the LLM steps (the generated form and the results display) so
7	     a malformed or hallucinated result is caught and retried, and never reaches the user or a
8	     downstream Apify call. (The agent's recommendation is validated in agent/discovery.py.)
9	
10	Each validator returns a list of human-readable error strings; an empty list means valid.
11	Keeping these deterministic (and cheap) is what lets us retry only when necessary and fall
12	back without another model call.
13	"""
14	from __future__ import annotations
15	
16	from typing import Any
17	
18	MAX_NEED_LEN = 500
19	MIN_NEED_LEN = 3
20	
21	
22	# --------------------------- input validation ---------------------------
23	def clean_need(need: str) -> str:
24	    return " ".join((need or "").split()).strip()
25	
26	
27	def validate_need(need: str) -> list[str]:
28	    errors: list[str] = []
29	    n = clean_need(need)
30	    if len(n) < MIN_NEED_LEN:
31	        errors.append("Please describe what data you're looking for.")
32	    if len(n) > MAX_NEED_LEN:
33	        errors.append(f"That's a bit long — please keep it under {MAX_NEED_LEN} characters.")
34	    return errors
35	
36	
37	def schema_props(detail: dict) -> dict:
38	    return (detail.get("inputSchema") or {}).get("properties") or {}
39	
40	
41	def required_keys(detail: dict) -> set[str]:
42	    return set((detail.get("inputSchema") or {}).get("required") or [])
43	
44	
45	def validate_run_input(values: dict, detail: dict) -> list[str]:
46	    """Check required inputs are present before running a source. Returns the schema keys
47	    that are missing (caller maps them to friendly labels)."""
48	    props = schema_props(detail)
49	    required = required_keys(detail)
50	    missing: list[str] = []
51	    for key in required:
52	        if key not in props:
53	            continue
54	        val = values.get(key)
55	        if val in (None, "", [], {}):
56	            missing.append(key)
57	    return missing
58	
59	
60	# --------------------------- output validation ---------------------------
61	# Note: the agent's terminal action is validated by `_validate_submit` in agent/discovery.py,
62	# next to the graph it guards.
63	def validate_form(out: Any, props: dict) -> list[str]:
64	    """The generated form must have at least one field bound to a REAL input key."""
65	    fields = getattr(out, "fields", None) or []
66	    if not fields:
67	        return ["form has no fields"]
68	    bound = [f for f in fields if getattr(f, "schema_key", None) in props]
69	    if not bound:
70	        return ["no form field maps to a real input"]
71	    return []
72	
73	
74	def validate_display(out: Any, keys: list[str]) -> list[str]:
75	    """Display must summarise results and, where it names f
…[truncated 708 chars]…

[856] ASSISTANT  2026-10-12T16:29:23.154Z

I reviewed all five plus the agent's `_validate_submit`. Verdict: they're structural/factual and correctly leave judgment to the agent — **with one exception** worth fixing.

**Fine (structure/facts only, no judgment):**
- `validate_need` → text length. `validate_form` → a field binds to a *real* key (doesn't judge *which* fields — that's the LLM). `validate_display` → summary present + named fields *exist* (doesn't judge layout). `sanitize_items` → rows are dicts. `_validate_submit` → chosen id is *real* + required parts present (presence, not quality).

**Oversteps → fixing:** `validate_run_input` treats the actor's `required` list as hard-missing even when the schema provides a **default/prefill**. In that case the source fills the value itself, so blocking the run second-guesses what the source (and the agent's form design) already handle — and would wrongly 400 a run that'd succeed. The deterministic check should only flag a required input that has *no* fallback value anywhere.

[861] ASSISTANT  2026-10-12T16:29:44.992Z
==========================================================================================
Reviewed and fixed. Summary of the audit:

**All validators now stay on the deterministic side of the line** — they check structure and facts, never the agent's judgment:

| Validator | Checks (factual) | Leaves to the agent/LLM |
|---|---|---|
| `validate_need` | text length | — |
| `validate_run_input` | required inputs with no value **and no default** | which fields to show |
| `validate_form` | ≥1 field binds to a **real** schema key | *which* fields, labels, defaults |
| `validate_display` | summary exists; named fields **exist** in data | layout/field choice |
| `sanitize_items` | rows are dicts | — |
| `_validate_submit` (agent) | chosen id is **real**; name/headline/reasons present | *which* source, *quality* of reasoning |

**The one overstep I found and fixed:** `validate_run_input` used to flag any missing `required` field — even one the schema gives a `default`/`prefill`, which the source fills itself. That second-guessed the source and the agent's form design, and would have wrongly blocked a valid run. Now it only flags a required input that's genuinely blank *and* has no fallback.

Verified: a required field with no default is still flagged (`['q']`), but a required field with a default is not.

Note: none of the validators judge *goodness* — e.g. `_validate_submit` requires reasons to **exist** (a product rule: "a recommendation with no reasoning is not acceptable") but never scores whether they're good; that judgment stays entirely with the agent. Restart the backend to run the change live.

[866] USER  2026-10-12T16:32:44.580Z
==========================================================================================
⮜ TOOL RESULT:
1	# Data Finder
2	
3	A no-code product that lets a **marketer describe a data need in plain words** and walks them
4	to **real, readable data** — without ever seeing an API, scraper, token, or JSON. An AI agent
5	does the discovery; a clean interface delivers the result.
6	
7	> The words *Apify*, *actor*, *API*, *endpoint*, *token* and *schema* never appear in the UI.
8	
9	---
10	
11	## The journey
12	
13	**Describe** → **Clarify** (only questions that change the answer) → **Search** (live,
14	human-readable progress) → **Recommend** (best source + why, with honest alternatives) →
15	**Use** (a form auto-generated from that source's real inputs) → **Results** (cards, not JSON).
16	
17	## Stack
18	
19	| Part | Choice | Why |
20	|------|--------|-----|
21	| Agent | **LangGraph** autonomous tool-calling agent | A message-driven agent ⇄ tools loop where the LLM decides its own next action (search again, inspect, or conclude) — bounded by hard guardrails (see below). Not a fixed pipeline, not one prompt returning a list. |
22	| LLM | **Google Gemini** (`gemini-3.8-flash`) | Fast + cheap; the agent makes several tool-driven calls per request, so latency and cost compound. (Gemini 3.x needs `langchain-google-genai` 4.x — see `requirements.txt`.) |
23	| Data engine | **Apify, via its MCP server** (`https://mcp.apify.com`) | The backend is an MCP client; Apify is invisible and server-side only. No direct REST, no other providers. |
24	| Backend | **FastAPI** | Holds session state, streams progress over SSE, enforces caps. |
25	| Frontend | **React + Vite** | The five-screen journey with loading / empty / error states. |
26	
27	## Architecture
28	
29	```
30	Browser (React)                     FastAPI                         Apify MCP        Gemini
31	   │  POST /api/describe  ───────────▶ clarify agent ─────────────────────────────────▶ (questions)
32	   │  POST /api/recommend (SSE) ─────▶ autonomous LangGraph agent (agent ⇄ tools loop):
33	   │        ◀─ progress events ─────── LLM decides: search → inspect → … → submit
34	   │                                        │  search-actors / fetch-actor-details ──▶ Apify
35	   │                                        └  decisions + language ────────────────▶ Gemini
36	   │        ◀─ result (recommendation + generated form)
37	   │  POST /api/run ─────────────────▶ coerce inputs + cap → call-actor → dataset ──▶ Apify
38	   │        ◀─ normalized rows + a display spec (title/rating/image/link/…) ───────▶ Gemini
39	```
40	
41	The autonomous LangGraph loop (`backend/app/agent/discovery.py`) — the LLM drives it:
42	
43	```
44	          ┌───────────────── loop ─────────────────┐
45	          ▼                                         │
46	   agent ──tool calls?──▶ tools ──search / inspect──┘
47	     │  │                   │
48	     │  │                   └─(valid submit · or budget hit)─▶ build_form ─▶ END
49	     │  └─plain text─▶ nudge once ─▶ agent
50	     └─(step/time/tool budget exceeded)─▶ build_form (determinist
…[truncated 8882 chars]…

Updated — the README now has a **"With another week"** section built around your five plans:

1. **More reliable, cost-optimised data flow** — cache source metadata/schemas, reuse one MCP session, model-tier routing (cheap model for search/clarify, stronger only for the decision), pre-run cost estimate, dedupe runs, and top-2 real test-runs as evidence when budget allows.

4. **More LLM-as-judge checks** — judges for recommendation evidence, form-fit, and result quality, each with a deterministic fallback.
5. **Evaluate other agent setups** — benchmark `create_react_agent`, a planner/executor split, and a multi-agent searcher+evaluator team against the current single-loop agent on cost/latency/quality.

Plus the eval harness kept as the "how I'd know it's good" piece.

Want me to start any of these now — the quickest high-value one would be **LangSmith tracing** (just a couple of env vars + wrapping, gives you the token/call breakdown you'll want for the cost work), or the **pre-run cost estimate**?

<pasted_content id="164b">
# Diagnosis Note
**AI Builder Task — Find the Best Data Source**

### 1. User and Actual Need
The target users are non-technical marketing professionals who know what information they want but do not know where or how to retrieve it.

Their actual need is not simply to find an API, but to obtain relevant, reliable, and actionable data through an intuitive interface without dealing with technical complexity.

### 2. Main Challenges
The main challenge is translating natural-language requests into the user's actual data requirements, especially when requests are ambiguous or do not clearly match an available data source.

Another challenge is selecting the most suitable source based on real evidence, rather than relying only on the LLM's assumptions, while ensuring reliable execution and meaningful results.

### 3. Proposed Solution
I will build an end-to-end application consisting of a React frontend, a Python FastAPI backend, and a single Gemini-powered AI agent integrated with Apify.

The agent will use a well-defined system prompt and a tool-calling loop to clarify user needs, search available sources, compare candidates, and provide evidence-based recommendations.

The system will dynamically generate a user-friendly input form based on the selected source's actual schema and display results in a readable format.

I will implement a **Deterministic Validator** for input/output structure, required fields, and execution limits, alongside bounded Retry and Fallback mechanisms to handle failures and unsuitable sources.

### 4. Scope and Trade-offs
Given the two-hour constraint, I will focus on one complete working flow with real Apify data.

I will deliberately exclude LLM-as-a-Judge validation, advanced UI styling, complex schema support, and multi-agent architecture. Since the task is focused on a single domain, one agent with multiple tools should be sufficient.

### 5. Success Criteria
Success means a non-technical user can describe a need, understand why a source was recommended, run it, and receive relevant, readable results without writing code.

The system should also handle invalid inputs, failed executions, and missing results gracefully.
</pasted_content id="164b">

[963] ASSISTANT  2026-10-12T16:44:33.507Z
==========================================================================================
Added and pushed. The roadmap now has a concrete **Evaluation process** (replacing the one-line eval harness):

1. **Golden set** — ~15–20 representative needs across categories, each with expectations (should-clarify, acceptable source ids, required form fields).
2. **Deterministic stage checks** (pass/fail, no LLM) — clarify ask-vs-skip precision/recall, recommendation names a real non-deprecated source with reasons/alternatives, form fields bind to real keys + cover required inputs, run returns well-formed rows within cap/time.
3. **LLM-as-judge scoring** (offline, stronger model) on the task's own **1–4 rubric** — reasoning grounded in evidence, form usability, result relevance.
4. **Operational metrics** from LangSmith — tokens, cost, latency, tool-call count, retry rate, and fallback rate.
5. **Regression gate** — ship only if quality holds/improves without a cost blow-up; a few cases spot-checked by hand.

It ties into the LangSmith and prompt-improvement items so changes are measured end to end. Live at **https://github.com/shaima77/Task-Kadabra-AI** (README → "With another week").

1	# Diagnosis Note
2	**AI Builder Task — Find the Best Data Source**
3	
4	*Originally written before building; updated to reflect what was actually delivered.*
5	
6	### 1. User and Actual Need
7	The target users are non-technical marketing professionals who know what information they want
8	but do not know where or how to retrieve it.
9	
10	Their actual need is not simply to find an API, but to obtain relevant, reliable, and
11	actionable data through an intuitive interface without dealing with technical complexity.
12	
13	### 2. Main Challenges
14	The main challenge is translating natural-language requests into the user's actual data
15	requirements, especially when requests are ambiguous or do not clearly match an available data
16	source.
17	
18	Another challenge is selecting the most suitable source based on real evidence, rather than
19	relying only on the LLM's assumptions, while ensuring reliable execution and meaningful results.
20	
21	### 3. Solution (as built)
22	An end-to-end application consisting of a React frontend, a Python FastAPI backend, and a single
23	Gemini-powered **autonomous agent** (built with LangGraph) integrated with **Apify through its
24	hosted MCP server** — never the raw REST API, and never exposed to the browser.
25

27	sources, inspect the most promising candidates, compare them, and produce an evidence-based
28	recommendation with honest alternatives. A lightweight **LLM-as-judge** first decides whether
29	any clarifying question is even needed, and asks only when an answer would change the choice.
30	
31	The system dynamically generates a user-friendly input form from the selected source's **actual
32	input schema** and displays results in a readable card format.
33	
34	I implemented a **Deterministic Validator** for input/output structure, required fields, and
35	real source ids/keys, alongside bounded **Retry, Timeout, and Fallback** mechanisms to handle
36	failures and unsuitable sources. The agent's autonomy is held inside **hard guardrails** (a
37	tool whitelist with no "run" tool, step/tool/search budgets, a wall-clock deadline, and a
38	validated terminal action), and runs are cost-capped (return at the result cap, then abort).
39	
40	### 4. Scope and Trade-offs
41	Given the two-hour constraint, I focused on one complete working flow with real Apify data.
42	
43	One scope decision changed during the build: I **added a small LLM-as-judge** for the clarify
44	step, because it was cheap and directly improved the experience (no needless questions on
45	already-specific requests).
46	
47	I deliberately excluded a **multi-agent architecture**, persistent storage / auth / billing,
48	recurring or scheduled pulls, and observability
49	tooling (e.g. LangSmith). Since the task is focused on a single domain, one agent with multiple
50	tools is sufficient.
51	
52	### 5. Success Criteria
53	Success means a non-technical us
